Faithful linear and relational representations of diagram categories and monoids
James East, Marianne Johnson, Mark Kambites

Abstract
. We study representations of diagram categories by binary relations and matrices over rings and semirings. Our main result is a faithful involutive tensor representation of the partition category 𝒫 \mathcal{P} (and consequently of each partition monoid 𝒫 n \mathcal{P}_{n} ) by zero-one matrices over an arbitrary (additively) idempotent semiring. The dimensions of the matrices involved are powers of 2 2 , and we show that these are minimal with respect to faithful involutive tensor representations by matrices over any semiring. Intriguingly, these matrices encode the number of floating components formed when composing partitions, and can therefore be used to construct faithful representations of ( d d -)twisted partition categories 𝒫 Φ \mathcal{P}^{\Phi} and 𝒫 Φ , d \mathcal{P}^{\Phi,d} (and the respective twisted partition monoids 𝒫 n Φ \mathcal{P}_{n}^{\Phi} and 𝒫 n Φ , d \mathcal{P}_{n}^{\Phi,d} ) over rings of appropriate characteristic. We also give lower-dimensional involutive representations of the Brauer and Temperley–Lieb categories ℬ \mathcal{B} and 𝒯 ​ ℒ \mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L} . In the case of 𝒯 ​ ℒ \mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L} , the dimensions are given by Fibonacci numbers. Keywords : Diagram categories, diagram monoids, relation categories, relation monoids, linear categories, linear monoids, representations. MSC: 18M05, 20M20, 20M30, 20M50, 16S50, 05E10, 05E16.



1.
Introduction

Diagram algebras arise naturally in many areas of mathematics and science, and play important roles in statistical mechanics, biology, computer science, representation theory, logic, combinatorics, knot theory and more. These algebras have bases consisting of various kinds of set partitions, which are represented and multiplied diagrammatically. Key examples include Brauer algebras
[
8
]
, Temperley–Lieb algebras
[
63
]
and partition algebras
[
56
,
40
]
; for more on the background and applications, see the survey
[
57
]
. Early on, Jones and Kauffman recognised the importance of underlying diagram
monoids

[
38
,
39
,
40
,
41
,
42
,
43
,
44
]
, which underpin the structure of the algebras, and the link was made precise by Wilcox in his work on twisted semigroup algebras
[
64
]
; see also
[
35
]
. Diagram monoids and algebras occur as endomorphism monoids/algebras of associated diagram
categories
, studied for example in
[
2
,
11
,
45
,
57
,
56
,
31
,
54
]
.

Diagram monoids have also become a major theme in modern semigroup theory, due to their intrinsically intriguing algebraic and combinatorial properties, and striking parallels with transformation semigroups. For example, there are studies on Green’s relations
[
64
,
33
]
, presentations by generators and relations
[
49
,
18
,
19
,
53
,
17
]
, characterisation and enumeration of idempotents
[
13
,
14
]
, idempotent-generation
[
12
,
21
,
24
,
22
]
, congruences
[
26
,
28
,
29
,
30
]
, Krohn–Rhodes complexity
[
3
]
, semigroup identities
[
4
,
5
,
47
,
48
]
, and connections to category theory
[
23
,
27
]
.

In a different direction, the recent article
[
9
]
studied transformation representations of diagram monoids, in each case giving an explicit formula for the
transformation degree
, i.e. the minimum number of points on which the monoid acts faithfully by functions. For example, the partition monoid
\mathcal{P}_{n}
has degree
1+\frac{B(n+2)-B(n-1)+B(n)}{2}
, where
B(n)
is the
n
th Bell number.
Any transformation representation leads to a
linear
representation of the same degree, over any unital (semi)ring. In general, however, there can be faithful linear representations whose dimension is substantially lower than the transformation degree, and this is one of the main motivations for the present work. As a sample result, we show that there is a faithful
2^{n}
-dimensional representation of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
by matrices over any (additively) idempotent semiring. This representation also models the well-known (product-reversing) involution of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
as transposition of matrices, thereby retaining information that is lost by transformation representations (as full transformation monoids have no involutions).
Since matrices over the
Boolean
semiring can be interpreted as binary relations, we also obtain a faithful representation of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
by relations of degree
2^{n}
.
In fact, all of these monoid representations can be packaged into a single faithful representation of the partition
category

\mathcal{P}
in the full matrix category
\mathcal{M}
. This category representation contains much more information, as it is defined also on hom-sets
\mathcal{P}_{m,n}
with
m\not=n
. It is additionally compatible with natural
tensor
structures on
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{M}
, and has order-theoretic connections as well.
We remark that the relation-theoretic interpretation of our representation
\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{M}
seems to be closely related to a special case of a functor considered briefly in
[
16
]
.

Let us now give a fuller summary of the paper.
We begin in Section
2
by recalling the preliminary material we need, including the definitions of the partition category
\mathcal{P}
, the Brauer category
\mathcal{B}
and the Temperley–Lieb category
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}
, as well as their twisted counterparts.

Section
3
contains our main construction, which is a mapping

\varphi=\varphi_{K}:\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{M}(K):a\mapsto\overline{a},

where
K
is any (fixed) unital semiring, and a partition
a\in\mathcal{P}_{m,n}
is mapped to a
{2^{m}\times 2^{n}}
zero-one matrix
\overline{a}\in\mathcal{M}_{2^{m},2^{n}}(K)
determined by the ‘blocks’ of
a
; see Definition
3.1
. This map
\varphi
is always injective, involutive and preserves the tensor operations; it is a morphism if and only if
K
is an
(additively) idempotent
semiring; see Proposition
3.3
, Theorem
3.11
and Remark
3.13
. The proof of Theorem
3.11
involves an analysis of what we call
intertwining sets
; see Definition
3.6
and Lemma
3.10
. It follows that for each fixed
n
the partition monoid
\mathcal{P}_{n}
embeds into the matrix monoid
\mathcal{M}_{2^{n}}(K)
over any unital idempotent semiring
K
. We conclude Section
3
by showing that the dimensions of the matrices in the image of
\varphi
are minimal with respect to faithful involutive tensor representations by matrices over
any
semiring; see Theorem
3.14
.

In Section
4
we adapt our mapping
\varphi
to construct a minimal faithful involutive tensor representation of the
twisted
partition category
\mathcal{P}^{\Phi}
over any semiring of characteristic
0
, leading also to faithful representations of the twisted partition monoids
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\Phi}
; see Theorem
4.1
. This is possible because of the underlying combinatorial properties of the intertwining sets mentioned above, and to their connection with ‘float counting’ parameters.
Theorem
4.3
provides a corresponding result for the
d
-twisted
categories
\mathcal{P}^{\Phi,d}
(and
d
-twisted partition monoids
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\Phi,d}
), involving a similar construction over rings of characteristic
2^{d+1}
. When
K
is a field of characteristic
0
, we show in Theorem
4.4
that the associated
\mathcal{P}^{\Phi}
-module
\mathcal{V}
(consisting of disjoint copies of
2^{n}
-dimensional vector spaces over
K
) decomposes as a direct sum of two irreducible submodules,
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{V}^{+}\oplus\mathcal{V}^{-}
, although neither
\mathcal{V}^{+}
nor
\mathcal{V}^{-}
is faithful; see Remark
4.6
.

In Sections
5
and
6
, we drop the requirement that our representations respect the tensor structure and show how the above representation of
\mathcal{P}
induces smaller degree faithful involutive representations of the subcategories
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}
(and hence of the submonoids
\mathcal{B}_{n}
and
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
); see Theorems
5.2
,
6.1
and
6.14
. In particular, a Temperley–Lieb diagram
a\in\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{m,n}
is faithfully represented as an
f_{m}\times f_{n}
matrix, where
f_{n}
is the
n
th Fibonacci number.

Section
7
explores connections to representations of diagram
algebras
and associated
linear
diagram categories. In particular, our representation of the twisted category
\mathcal{P}^{\Phi}
leads to matrix representations of the linear partition categories
\mathcal{P}(K,2)
, and hence of the partition algebras
\mathcal{P}_{n}(K,2)
, where
K
is a ring of characteristic
0
. These are not faithful (see Example
7.2
), and neither are the restrictions to the Brauer algebras (see Example
7.3
). However, the restrictions to the Temperley–Lieb algebras coincide with well-known representations
[
8
]
, which
are
known to be faithful when
K
is a field
[
41
,
15
]
. We give a simple argument to deduce that our representation of the full linear Temperley–Lieb category
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}(K,2)
is itself faithful; see Theorem
7.4
.

Finally, Section
8
contains a number of open questions for future research. A natural problem is to determine the
minimum
dimensions of faithful linear representations of the (twisted) diagram categories and monoids. In particular, it is intriguing to ask whether or when the faithful, involutive representations we give here are minimal. For example, our representation of the partition monoid
\mathcal{P}_{2}
over the Boolean semiring has degree
2^{2}=4
; it can be shown computationally with GAP
[
34
,
60
]
that this monoid cannot be faithfully represented in degree
3
, but we have no theoretical method that would allow us to establish corresponding lower bounds in higher rank cases where computational approaches are infeasible.

2.
Preliminaries

We now gather the definitions and results we need on general categories and semirings, as well as specific categories of matrices and diagrams/partitions. For further details and background, see for example
[
62
,
52
,
20
,
37
,
36
,
30
]
. We denote the sets of integers and natural numbers by
\mathbb{Z}=\{0,\pm 1,\pm 2,\ldots\}
and
\mathbb{N}=\{0,1,2,\ldots\}
, respectively.
For a natural number
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we write
[n]=\{1,\ldots,n\}
, interpreting this to be empty when
n=0
. Throughout, maps will typically be written on the right and hence composed reading left to right.

2.1.
Categories

All categories we consider are
small
, meaning that their morphisms form a set. Formally, a category is a tuple
(\mathcal{C},O,{\bf d},{\bf r},\circ)
, where:

•

\mathcal{C}
is the set of morphisms,

•

O
is the set of objects,

•

{\bf d}:\mathcal{C}\to O
and
{\bf r}:\mathcal{C}\to O
are the domain and range maps, and

•

\circ
is the composition operation of morphisms, where
a\circ b
is defined (for
a,b\in\mathcal{C}
) if and only if
{\bf r}(a)={\bf d}(b)
, in which case
{\bf d}(a\circ b)={\bf d}(a)
and
{\bf r}(a\circ b)={\bf r}(b)
,

with composition satisfying the associative law where defined, and where for each object
s\in O
there exists an
identity morphism
at
s
denoted
\iota_{s}
with
{\bf d}(\iota_{s})={\bf r}(\iota_{s})=s
and such that
\iota_{s}\circ a=a
and
b\circ\iota_{s}=b
wherever defined. We typically identify the category
(\mathcal{C},O,{\bf d},{\bf r},\circ)
with the underlying (morphism) set
\mathcal{C}
, and use juxtaposition to denote composition:
ab=a\circ b
. For objects
s,t\in O
, we write

\mathcal{C}_{s,t}=\{a\in\mathcal{C}:{\bf d}(a)=s,\ {\bf r}(a)=t\}

for the set of all morphisms
s\to t
, and refer to such sets as hom-sets. Note that if
s
and
t
are distinct there is no requirement that
\mathcal{C}_{s,t}
be non-empty; for
s=t
we have
\iota_{s}\in\mathcal{C}_{s,s}
. We write
\mathcal{C}_{s}=\mathcal{C}_{s,s}
for the endomorphism monoid of the object
s\in O
.

An
involution
on a category
\mathcal{C}
is a map
\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}:a\mapsto a^{*}
satisfying the following for all
a,b\in\mathcal{C}
:

•

{\bf d}(a^{*})={\bf r}(a)
and
{\bf r}(a^{*})={\bf d}(a)
,

•

a^{**}=a
,

•

(ab)^{*}=b^{*}a^{*}
when
{\bf r}(a)={\bf d}(b)
.

It is an easy exercise to deduce that the above axioms imply also
that
\iota_{s}^{*}=\iota_{s}
for each object
s
.
An
involutive category
is a category with a specified involution. These are also called
dagger categories
or
*
-categories
in the literature. A
regular
*
-category
is an involutive category
\mathcal{C}
for which
a=aa^{*}a
for all
a\in\mathcal{C}
; it follows then that also
a^{*}=a^{*}aa^{*}
, so that
a^{*}
is a
(generalised) inverse
of
a
.

The categories of interest in this paper are
strict tensor categories
, meaning that the object and morphism sets have additional (totally defined) binary operations, satisfying natural conditions. In all cases we consider, the object set
O
of
\mathcal{C}
will be either
(\mathbb{N},+)
or
(\mathbb{N},\cdot)
, the additive or multiplicative monoids over the natural numbers
{\mathbb{N}=\{0,1,2,\ldots\}}
, in which case the corresponding
tensor
operation on
\mathcal{C}
will be denoted
\oplus
or
\otimes
, respectively.
When
O=(\mathbb{N},+)
the axioms are as follows:

•

{\bf d}(a\oplus b)={\bf d}(a)+{\bf d}(b)
and
{\bf r}(a\oplus b)={\bf r}(a)+{\bf r}(b)
,

•

a\oplus(b\oplus c)=(a\oplus b)\oplus c
,

•

a\oplus\iota_{0}=a=\iota_{0}\oplus a
,

•

\iota_{m}\oplus\iota_{n}=\iota_{m+n}
,

•

(a\circ b)\oplus(c\circ d)=(a\oplus c)\circ(b\oplus d)
if
{\bf r}(a)={\bf d}(b)
and
{\bf r}(c)={\bf d}(d)
.

The axioms are analogous in the case
O=(\mathbb{N},\cdot)
; we replace
\oplus
and
+
by
\otimes
and
\cdot
in each law, and
\iota_{0}
by
\iota_{1}
in the third. The term ‘strict’ refers to the fact that we have equality in the above laws, rather than isomorphism, as for more general tensor categories. Since we only ever refer to strict tensor categories, we drop ‘strict’ henceforth.

An
involutive tensor category
is simultaneously an involutive and tensor category, in which
(a\oplus b)^{*}=a^{*}\oplus b^{*}
or
(a\otimes b)^{*}=a^{*}\otimes b^{*}
, as appropriate.

A category
representation
is a functor
\phi:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
between categories. We say
\phi
is:

•

faithful
if it is injective on objects and morphisms,

•

involutive
if
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
are both involutive categories, and
\phi
preserves the involutions,

•

a
tensor representation
, if
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
are both tensor categories, and
\phi
preserves the tensor operations.

2.2.
Semirings

A
(unital) semiring
is a tuple
(K,+,\cdot,0,1)
, such that:

•

(K,+,0)
is a commutative monoid, with
+
referred to as addition,

•

(K,\cdot,1)
is a monoid, with
\cdot
referred to as multiplication,

•

multiplication distributes over addition, and

•

0
is a multiplicative zero element.

We typically identify a semiring with its underlying set
K
, and we always assume that
K
is non-trivial, in the sense that
0\not=1
.
We say that a semiring
K
:

•

is
commutative
if the multiplication of
K
is commutative,

•

is
(additively) idempotent
if
1+1=1
(or equivalently if
\alpha+\alpha=\alpha
for all
\alpha\in K
),

•

has
characteristic 0
if
1
generates an infinite subsemigroup under addition.

The prototypical example of an idempotent semiring is the
Boolean semiring
,
\mathbb{B}=\{0,1\}
, in which
1+1=1
(and all other additions and multiplications follow from the axioms). Another important and widely studied example is the
tropical
(or
max-plus
)
semiring
(see for example
[
51
]
). The natural numbers under ordinary addition and multiplication is a semiring of characteristic
0
.

In an arbitrary semiring of characteristic
0
, we shall identify a natural number
n
with the element
\underbrace{1+\cdots+1}_{n}
in
K
, interpreting this as the zero of
K
when
n=0
. If
K
is a semiring with the property that
\underbrace{1+\cdots+1}_{d}=0
for some
d\geq 2
, then clearly
1
(and hence, by distributivity, every element of
K
) has an additive inverse; thus in this case
K
is a ring. If
d
is the least such positive integer, then
K
is a ring of characteristic
d
. In a ring of characteristic
d
, each integer
0\leq n<d
will be identified with
\underbrace{1+\cdots+1}_{n}
in
K
.

2.3.
Categories of matrices

Throughout this section we fix a (non-trivial unital) semiring
K
. For sets
I
and
J
, we write
\mathcal{M}_{I,J}
for the set of all
I\times J
matrices over
K
; later we write
\mathcal{M}_{I,J}(K)
when we wish to emphasise the semiring
K
, with an analogous convention for other sets of matrices. For
a\in\mathcal{M}_{I,J}
, and for
i\in I
and
j\in J
, we denote the
(i,j)
th entry of
a
by
a_{i,j}
, and write
a=(a_{i,j})_{i\in I,j\in J}
, or simply
a=(a_{i,j})
if the indexing sets are understood from context. When
I
or
J
is the empty set, we assume that
\mathcal{M}_{I,J}
consists of a unique
I\times J
empty matrix.

If
I=[m]=\{1,\ldots,m\}
and
J=[n]=\{1,\ldots,n\}
for natural numbers
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
, we simply write
\mathcal{M}_{m,n}=\mathcal{M}_{[m],[n]}
. The set

\mathcal{M}=\bigcup_{m,n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{M}_{m,n}

is an involutive tensor category with object set
(\mathbb{N},\cdot)
, where composition and involution are given by ordinary matrix multiplication and transpose, the latter being denoted
a\mapsto a^{T}
. The tensor operation is the
Kronecker product

\otimes
, whose definition we recall below. For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the endomorphism monoid
\mathcal{M}_{n}=\mathcal{M}_{n,n}
is the full linear monoid of degree
n
over
K
; its identity is the usual
n\times n
identity matrix, which we denote by
I_{n}
. Note that
\mathcal{M}_{n,0}
and
\mathcal{M}_{0,n}
respectively consist solely of the unique
n\times 0
or
0\times n
empty matrix, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

In the special case that
K=\mathbb{B}
is the Boolean semiring, the category
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{B})
is isomorphic to the
relation category

\mathcal{R}
. The latter has object set
\mathbb{N}
, and the morphism set
\mathcal{R}_{m,n}
consists of all relations
[m]\to[n]
, i.e. all subsets of
[m]\times[n]
. The rule for composing relations
\alpha\in\mathcal{R}_{m,n}
and
\beta\in\mathcal{R}_{n,t}
is

\alpha\beta=\big\{(i,j)\in[m]\times[t]:(i,x)\in\alpha,\ (x,j)\in\beta\ (\exists x\in[n])\big\}.

The involution is given by
\alpha^{*}=\{(y,x):(x,y)\in\alpha\}
.
The above-mentioned isomorphism
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{B})\to\mathcal{R}
sends a matrix
a\in\mathcal{M}_{m,n}(\mathbb{B})
to the relation
{\big\{(i,j)\in[m]\times[n]:a_{i,j}=1\big\}}
.

Definition 2.1
.

For matrices
A=(a_{i,j})\in\mathcal{M}_{I,J}
and
B=(b_{u,v})\in\mathcal{M}_{U,V}
, the
Kronecker product

A\otimes B\in\mathcal{M}_{I\times U,J\times V}
is the matrix with entry
a_{i,j}b_{u,v}
in row-
(i,u)
, column-
(j,v)
.

For example, if
I=[m]
,
J=[n]
,
U=[s]
and
V=[t]
for natural numbers
{m,n,s,t\in\mathbb{N}}
, then the bijections

\displaystyle[m]\times[s]\to[ms]

\displaystyle:(i,u)\mapsto(i-1)s+u

\displaystyle\text{and}\qquad[n]\times[t]\to[nt]

\displaystyle:(j,v)\mapsto(j-1)t+v

allow us to regard
A\otimes B
as in Definition
2.1
as the
ms\times nt
matrix

A\otimes B=\begin{pmatrix}a_{1,1}B&\cdots&a_{1,n}B\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
a_{m,1}B&\cdots&a_{m,n}B\end{pmatrix}\in\mathcal{M}_{ms,nt}.

(If any of
m
,
n
,
s
or
t
is
0
, then
A\otimes B
is an empty matrix of appropriate dimensions.) It is this formulation of the Kronecker product
\otimes
that gives
\mathcal{M}
its tensor category structure.

As another example, consider
A=(a_{X,Y})\in\mathcal{M}_{2^{[m]},2^{[n]}}
and
{B=(b_{U,V})\in\mathcal{M}_{2^{[s]},2^{[t]}}}
for natural numbers
m,n,s,t\in\mathbb{N}
. Here
2^{[m]}=\{X:X\subseteq[m]\}
denotes the power set of
[m]
, and so on. The bijections between power sets

\displaystyle 2^{[m]}\times 2^{[s]}\to 2^{[m+s]}

\displaystyle:(X,U)\mapsto X\cup(U+m)

\displaystyle\text{and}\qquad 2^{[n]}\times 2^{[t]}\to 2^{[n+t]}

\displaystyle:(Y,V)\mapsto Y\cup(V+n)

allow us to regard
A\otimes B
as in Definition
2.1
as a
2^{[m+s]}\times 2^{[n+t]}
matrix. (In the above we write
U+m=\{u+m:u\in U\}
and
V+n=\{v+n:v\in V\}
.) For
P\subseteq[m+s]
and
Q\subseteq[n+t]
, there exist unique
X\subseteq[m]
,
Y\subseteq[n]
,
U\subseteq[s]
and
V\subseteq[t]
such that
P=X\cup(U+m)
and
Q=Y\cup(V+n)
, giving

(2.1)

(A\otimes B)_{P,Q}=a_{X,Y}b_{U,V}.

2.4.
Matrix representations and effective dimensions

A matrix representation of a category
\mathcal{C}
is a functor

\phi:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{M}(K)

for some semiring
K
. The main results of this paper concern matrix representations of tensor categories that are faithful, involutive, and in some cases respect the tensor structure. The ‘efficiency’ of such a representation can be measured by looking at the dimensions of the matrices in the image. Let the object set of
\mathcal{C}
be
O
, and for each
s\in O
write
d_{s}=s\phi
. Recall that, formally, we have defined the objects of
\mathcal{M}(K)
to be natural numbers, so that each
d_{s}\in\mathbb{N}
. Note that
\iota_{s}\phi=I_{d_{s}}
for all
s\in O
. It follows that
\phi
maps a hom-set
\mathcal{C}_{s,t}
into
\mathcal{M}_{d_{s},d_{t}}
. In particular,
\phi
maps each endomorphism monoid
\mathcal{C}_{s}
to the monoid
\mathcal{M}_{d_{s}}
of
d_{s}\times d_{s}
matrices over
K
. Consequently, if
\phi
is faithful, then each
\mathcal{C}_{s}
embeds in
\mathcal{M}_{d_{s}}
, in which case we have upper bounds for the
effective dimensions
:

(2.2)

\dim_{K}(M):=\min\big\{d:M\text{ embeds in }\mathcal{M}_{d}(K)\big\}\leq d_{s}\qquad\text{for any submonoid $M$ of $\mathcal{C}_{s}$.}

Our main results below give faithful matrix representations of certain diagram categories (to be defined shortly). For each category
\mathcal{C}
considered we also state the dimension of the image of each endomorphism monoid
\mathcal{C}_{s}
, which leads to upper bounds on effective dimensions of (submonoids) of the corresponding diagram monoids, as in (
2.2
). See in particular Sections
3.4
and
8
below for further discussion.

2.5.
Diagram categories and monoids

For
A\subseteq\mathbb{N}
we write
{A^{\prime}=\{x^{\prime}:x\in A\}}
and
A^{\prime\prime}=\{x^{\prime\prime}:x\in A\}
for two distinguished disjoint copies of
A
.

Definition 2.2
.

For natural numbers
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
we write
\mathcal{P}_{m,n}
for the set of all set partitions of
[m]\cup[n]^{\prime}
. The
partition category
has object set
\mathbb{N}
, and morphism set

\mathcal{P}=\bigcup_{m,n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{P}_{m,n},

under an operation that will be defined shortly.

For a partition
a\in\mathcal{P}_{m,n}
we write
{\bf d}(a)=m
and
{\bf r}(a)=n
. We refer to the elements of
a
as the
blocks
of
a
, or as
a
-blocks
. We identify
a
with any graph on vertex set
[m]\cup[n]^{\prime}
whose connected components are the
a
-blocks. Such graphs are drawn in the plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with vertex
x\in[m]
in position
(x,1)
, and vertex
x^{\prime}\in[n]^{\prime}
in position
(x,0)
; thus, the vertices from
[m]
form a single upper row ordered consecutively from left to right, and likewise the vertices from
[n]^{\prime}
form a single lower row. All such graphs are drawn with all edges contained within the region

(2.3)

\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:0\leq y\leq 1\}.

(This is primarily important for planarity considerations below.) See Figure
1
for some examples.

The composition/product of partitions
a,b\in\mathcal{P}
is defined if and only if
{\bf r}(a)={\bf d}(b)
. So consider composable partitions
a\in\mathcal{P}_{m,n}
and
b\in\mathcal{P}_{n,t}
, for some
m,n,t\in\mathbb{N}
. Informally,
ab
is calculated by first ‘glueing’ the bottom of
a
to the top of
b
, and then determining the connections among the top and bottom rows of vertices in this graph.
To formalise this, we first define three further graphs:

•

a^{\downarrow}
, with vertex set
[m]\cup[n]^{\prime\prime}
, obtained from
a
by changing each lower vertex
x^{\prime}
to
x^{\prime\prime}
,

•

b^{\uparrow}
, with vertex set
[n]^{\prime\prime}\cup[t]^{\prime}
, obtained from
b
by changing each upper vertex
x
to
x^{\prime\prime}
, and

•

\Pi(a,b)
, with vertex set
[m]\cup[n]^{\prime\prime}\cup[t]^{\prime}
, whose edge set is the union of the edge sets of
a^{\downarrow}
and
b^{\uparrow}
.

The graph
\Pi(a,b)
is called the product graph of
a
and
b
; it is drawn with vertices from
[n]^{\prime\prime}
in a middle row (on the line
y=\frac{1}{2}
). The product
ab\in\mathcal{P}_{m,t}
is then defined so that
{x,y\in[m]\cup[t]^{\prime}}
belong to the same
ab
-block if and only if
x
and
y
are contained in the same connected component of
\Pi(a,b)
. A sample product is considered in Example
2.4
.

The endomorphism monoids in
\mathcal{P}
are the
partition monoids

\mathcal{P}_{n}=\mathcal{P}_{n,n}
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
. The identity element of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
is the partition

\iota_{n}=\big\{\{1,1^{\prime}\},\{2,2^{\prime}\},\ldots,\{n,n^{\prime}\}\big\}={\lower 6.02773pt\hbox{
\hbox to29.02pt{\vbox to20.49pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-6.82875pt\lower-1.70715pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}}.

(Note that
\iota_{0}
is the empty partition, the unique element of
\mathcal{P}_{0}
.) The group of units of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
(i.e. the automorphism group at the object
n
) is the symmetric group
\mathcal{S}_{n}
. Here a permutation
\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{n}
is identified with the partition
{\big\{\{i,(i\pi)^{\prime}\}:i\in[n]\big\}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}
.

Definition 2.3
.

Consider a pair of composable partitions
{a\in\mathcal{P}_{m,n}}
and
b\in\mathcal{P}_{n,t}
. The product graph
\Pi(a,b)
may contain connected components that lie entirely in
[n]^{\prime\prime}
; viewed as subsets of vertices these components do not depend upon the choice of graphical representation of
a
and
b
. We refer to these as
floating components
, and write
\Phi(a,b)
to denote the number of them.

Example 2.4
.

Computation of the product of

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle=\big\{\{1,4\},\{2,3,4^{\prime},5^{\prime}\},\{1^{\prime},2^{\prime},6^{\prime}\},\{3^{\prime}\}\big\}\in\mathcal{P}_{4,6}

\displaystyle\text{and}\qquad b

\displaystyle=\big\{\{1,2\},\{3,4,1^{\prime}\},\{5,4^{\prime},5^{\prime}\},\{6\},\{2^{\prime},3^{\prime}\}\big\}\in\mathcal{P}_{6,5}

is illustrated in Figure
1
using graphical representations of
a
and
b
. The product graph
\Pi(a,b)
is constructed by identifying each vertex
i^{\prime}
in
a
with the vertex
i
in
b
, and relabelling this as
i^{\prime\prime}
. The product

\displaystyle ab

\displaystyle=\big\{\{1,4\},\{2,3,1^{\prime},4^{\prime},5^{\prime}\},\{2^{\prime},3^{\prime}\}\big\}\in\mathcal{P}_{4,5}

has blocks corresponding to the non-empty intersections of the connected components of
\Pi(a,b)
with
[4]\cup[5]^{\prime}
. Notice that one of the connected components of
\Pi(a,b)
, namely
\{1^{\prime\prime},2^{\prime\prime},6^{\prime\prime}\}
, lies within
[6]^{\prime\prime}
; this is a floating component, and since it is the only such component we have
\Phi(a,b)=1
.

a=
b=
=ab

Figure 1
.
Partitions
a\in\mathcal{P}_{4,6}
and
b\in\mathcal{P}_{6,5}
and their product
ab\in\mathcal{P}_{4,5}
, with the product graph
\Pi(a,b)
in the middle. This has one floating component, namely
\{1^{\prime\prime},2^{\prime\prime},6^{\prime\prime}\}
, so
\Phi(a,b)=1
in this case.

Definition 2.5
.

The blocks of a partition
a\in\mathcal{P}_{m,n}
can in general be of three types. We say that
X\in a
is:

•

an
upper
a
-block
if
X\subseteq[m]
,

•

a
lower
a
-block
if
X\subseteq[n]^{\prime}
,

•

a
transversal
a
-block
(or just a
transversal
) otherwise, i.e. if
X
contains at least one element of
[m]
and at least one element of
[n]^{\prime}
.

We write
a=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{1}&\cdots&B_{r}&D_{1}&\cdots&D_{t}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
to indicate that
a
has transversals
A_{i}\cup B_{i}^{\prime}
(
1\leq i\leq r
), upper blocks
C_{i}
(
1\leq i\leq s
) and lower blocks
D_{i}^{\prime}
(
1\leq i\leq t
). Note that any of
r
,
s
or
t
could be
0
; we have
r=s=t=0
if and only if
m=n=0
, in which case
a=\emptyset
is the unique element of
\mathcal{P}_{0}
.

Definition 2.6
.

The (co)domain, (co)kernel and rank of a partition
a\in\mathcal{P}_{m,n}
are defined by:

•

{\rm dom}(a)=\{x\in[m]:x\text{ is contained in a transversal }a\mbox{-block}\}
,

•

{\rm codom}(a)=\{x\in[n]:x^{\prime}\text{ is contained in a transversal }a\mbox{-block}\}
,

•

{\rm ker}(a)=\{(x,y)\in[m]\times[m]:x\mbox{ and }y\mbox{ are in the same }a\mbox{-block}\}
,

•

{\rm coker}(a)=\{(x,y)\in[n]\times[n]:x^{\prime}\mbox{ and }y^{\prime}\mbox{ are in the same }a\mbox{-block}\}
,

•

{\rm rank}(a)
is the number of transversal
a
-blocks.

Note that
\operatorname{dom}(a)
and
\operatorname{codom}(a)
are respectively subsets of
[m]
and
[n]
;
\ker(a)
and
\operatorname{coker}(a)
are respectively equivalences on
[m]
and
[n]
; and
0\leq\operatorname{rank}(a)\leq\min(m,n)
is an integer. If
a=\Bigl(\hskip-4.2679pt\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{@{\hskip 3pt} c *{\theincols}{|c} @{\hskip 3pt} }.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}&C_{1}&\cdots&C_{s}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\\
\hline\cr.0pt\lx@intercol\hfil B_{1}&\cdots&B_{r}&D_{1}&\cdots&D_{t}\hfil\hskip 3.0pt\end{array}\normalsize\hskip-4.2679pt\Bigr)
, then

\displaystyle\operatorname{dom}(a)

\displaystyle=A_{1}\cup\cdots\cup A_{r},

\displaystyle[m]/\ker(a)

\displaystyle=\{A_{1},\ldots,A_{r},C_{1},\ldots,C_{s}\},

\displaystyle\operatorname{rank}(a)

\displaystyle=r,

\displaystyle\operatorname{codom}(a)

\displaystyle=B_{1}\cup\cdots\cup B_{r},

\displaystyle[n]/\operatorname{coker}(a)

\displaystyle=\{B_{1},\ldots,B_{r},D_{1},\ldots,D_{t}\}.

Example 2.7
.

The partition
a\in\mathcal{P}_{4,6}
illustrated in Figure
1
has one upper block, two lower blocks and one transversal. Thus
{\rm rank}(a)=1
. Moreover,
{\rm dom}(a)=\{2,3\}
,
{\rm codom}(a)=\{4,5\}
,
{\rm ker}(a)
is the equivalence relation on
[4]
with classes
\{1,4\}
and
\{2,3\}
, and
{\rm coker}(a)
is the equivalence relation on
[6]
with classes
\{1,2,6\}
,
\{3\}
and
\{4,5\}
.

Definition 2.8
.

The partition category
\mathcal{P}
is a regular
*
-category, with respect to the involution
∗
given by applying the relabelling
i\leftrightarrow i^{\prime}
. This corresponds to a vertical reflection of a graphical representative. For example, with
a\in\mathcal{P}_{4,6}
as in Figure
1
, we have

}}\in\mathcal{P}_{6,4}
.

Definition 2.9
.

The partition category
\mathcal{P}
is a tensor category, under a horizontal stacking operation denoted by
\oplus
. Here for
a\in\mathcal{P}_{m,n}
and
b\in\mathcal{P}_{s,t}
we define
a\oplus b\in\mathcal{P}_{m+s,n+t}
to be the result of placing
a
and
b
side-by-side, with
a
on the left. For example,

}}

Formally, every
a
-block is an
(a\oplus b)
-block, while every
b
-block
X\cup Y^{\prime}
(with
X
or
Y
possibly empty) gives rise to the
(a\oplus b)
-block
(X+m)\cup(Y+n)^{\prime}
.

Definition 2.10
.

The
Brauer category

\mathcal{B}
is the subcategory of
\mathcal{P}
consisting of those partitions in which each block has size
2
. The
Temperley–Lieb category

\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}
is the subcategory of
\mathcal{B}
consisting of all planar Brauer partitions, i.e. those that can be represented by a graph with no crossings, remembering that all edges must be drawn within the region specified in (
2.3
). For example, we have

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}}\in\mathcal{B}\setminus\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}.

Since
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}
are closed under both
∗
and
\oplus
, both are involutive tensor categories.
Endomorphism monoids in
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}
are Brauer monoids
\mathcal{B}_{n}
and Temperley–Lieb monoids
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
, respectively. Temperley–Lieb monoids are also known as Jones monoids in the literature.

Note that for
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
we have
\mathcal{B}_{m,n}\not=\emptyset\ \Leftrightarrow\ \mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{m,n}\not=\emptyset\ \Leftrightarrow\ m\equiv n\bmod 2
. Thus,
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}
both decompose as the disjoint unions of two subcategories:

\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}_{\operatorname{even}}\sqcup\mathcal{B}_{\operatorname{odd}}\and\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{even}}\sqcup\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{odd}},

where

\mathcal{B}_{\operatorname{even}}=\bigcup_{m,n\in 2\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{B}_{m,n}\and\mathcal{B}_{\operatorname{odd}}=\bigcup_{m,n\in 2\mathbb{N}+1}\mathcal{B}_{m,n},

with analogous meanings for
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{even}}
and
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{odd}}
. Note that
\mathcal{B}_{\operatorname{even}}
and
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{even}}
are closed under
\oplus
, but
\mathcal{B}_{\operatorname{odd}}
and
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{odd}}
are not; all four of these subcategories are closed under
∗
.

2.6.
Twisted diagram categories and monoids

Twisted diagram monoids incorporate the product of an underlying diagram monoid (such as
\mathcal{P}_{n}
,
\mathcal{B}_{n}
or
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
) as well as the numbers
\Phi(a,b)
from Definition
2.3
. The definition extends without serious modification to twisted diagram
categories
:

Definition 2.11
.

The
twisted partition category
is the involutive tensor category with object set
\mathbb{N}
, morphism set

\mathcal{P}^{\Phi}=\mathbb{N}\times\mathcal{P}=\{(i,a):i\in\mathbb{N},\ a\in\mathcal{P}\},

and operations given by:

•

{\bf d}(i,a)={\bf d}(a)
and
{\bf r}(i,a)={\bf r}(a)
,

•

(i,a)(j,b)=(i+j+\Phi(a,b),ab)
when
{\bf r}(a)={\bf d}(b)
,

•

(i,a)^{*}=(i,a^{*})
,

•

(i,a)\oplus(j,b)=(i+j,a\oplus b)
.

Associativity of the composition follows from the ‘twisting identity’

\Phi(a,b)+\Phi(ab,c)=\Phi(a,bc)+\Phi(b,c)\qquad\text{for composable $a,b,c\in\mathcal{P}$,}

as observed in
[
64
,
33
]
. This identity was proved in the monoid case in
[
33
, Lemma 4.1]
, but the proof works without significant modification for the category
\mathcal{P}
.
Hom-sets in
\mathcal{P}^{\Phi}
have the form
\mathcal{P}_{m,n}^{\Phi}=\mathbb{N}\times\mathcal{P}_{m,n}
,
and endomorphism monoids are the
twisted partition monoids

\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\Phi}=\mathcal{P}_{n,n}^{\Phi}
. The identity of
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\Phi}
is
(0,\iota_{n})
.

Definition 2.12
.

For each natural number
d\in\mathbb{N}
, the
d
-twisted partition category

\mathcal{P}^{\Phi,d}
is the involutive tensor category with object set
\mathbb{N}
, and (finite) morphism sets

\mathcal{P}_{m,n}^{\Phi,d}=\big(\{0,1,\ldots,d\}\times\mathcal{P}_{m,n}\big)\cup\{\varnothing_{m,n}\}.

The operations are induced from those of
\mathcal{P}^{\Phi}
, with the morphisms
\varnothing_{m,n}
acting as zero elements that also occur when compositions or tensor sums of non-zero morphisms yield integer-coordinates greater than
d
. So we have:

\displaystyle(i,a)(j,b)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}(i+j+\Phi(a,b),ab)&\text{if ${\bf r}(a)={\bf d}(b)$ and $i+j+\Phi(a,b)\leq d$,}\\
\varnothing_{{\bf d}(a),{\bf r}(b)}&\text{if ${\bf r}(a)={\bf d}(b)$ and $i+j+\Phi(a,b)>d$,}\end{cases}

\displaystyle(i,a)\oplus(j,b)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}(i+j,a\oplus b)&\text{if $i+j\leq d$,}\\
\varnothing_{{\bf d}(a)+{\bf d}(b),{\bf r}(a)+{\bf r}(b)}&\text{if $i+j>d$,}\end{cases}

and also

\varnothing_{m,n}\circ\mathcal{P}_{n,t}^{\Phi,d}=\varnothing_{m,t}=\mathcal{P}_{m,n}^{\Phi,d}\circ\varnothing_{n,t}\and\varnothing_{m,n}\oplus\mathcal{P}_{s,t}^{\Phi,d}=\varnothing_{m+s,n+t}=\mathcal{P}_{m,n}^{\Phi,d}\oplus\varnothing_{s,t}.

The involution is given by

(i,a)^{*}=(i,a^{*})\and\varnothing_{m,n}^{*}=\varnothing_{n,m}.

One can think of
\mathcal{P}^{\Phi,d}
as a
Rees quotient
of
\mathcal{P}^{\Phi}
by the ideal
\{d+1,d+2,\ldots\}\times\mathcal{P}
. (For more details, and for the meaning of these terms, see
[
31
,
30
]
.)

Endomorphism monoids
\mathcal{P}_{n}^{\Phi,d}
are the
d
-twisted partition monoids; they were denoted
\mathcal{P}_{n,d}^{\Phi}
in
[
29
,
30
]
, but here we have placed the ‘
d
’ in a superscript to avoid confusion with hom-sets
\mathcal{P}_{m,n}^{\Phi}
in the category
\mathcal{P}^{\Phi}
.

Definition 2.13
.

One also of course has (
d
-)twisted Brauer and Temperley–Lieb categories, which we denote by
\mathcal{B}^{\Phi}
,
\mathcal{B}^{\Phi,d}
,
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}^{\Phi}
and
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}^{\Phi,d}
with the obvious meanings. Note that in the
d
-twisted categories
\mathcal{B}^{\Phi,d}
and
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}^{\Phi,d}
we only need zero morphisms
\varnothing_{m,n}
when
m\equiv n\bmod 2
.
Endomorphism monoids
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}^{\Phi}
are known in the literature as Kauffman monoids
[
7
,
5
,
50
,
47
,
12
]
.

Remark 2.14
.

One can replace
\mathbb{N}
by
\mathbb{Z}
in the definitions of
\mathcal{P}^{\Phi}
,
\mathcal{B}^{\Phi}
and
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}^{\Phi}
, to obtain twisted categories on underlying sets
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathcal{P}
,
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathcal{B}
and
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}
. This has proved fruitful in the monoid case in a number of studies
[
47
,
24
,
6
]
.

3.
Representing the partition category

This section is central to the rest of the paper, and contains our main result, Theorem
3.11
, which gives a faithful involutive tensor representation of the partition category
\mathcal{P}
in the matrix category
\mathcal{M}
over any (non-trivial) idempotent semiring. Sections
3.1
and
3.2
contain the relevant definitions and some preliminary lemmas, while Section
3.3
contains the theorem and its proof. Finally, in Section
3.4
we show that the dimensions of the matrices in the image of our representation are minimal for
any
faithful involutive tensor representation
\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{M}(K)
for
any
semiring
K
.

3.1.
A mapping to zero-one matrices

Fix a (non-trivial unital) semiring
K
, and let
{\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}(K)}
be the involutive tensor category of matrices over
K
. In all that follows, we shall view elements of
\mathcal{M}_{2^{m},2^{n}}=\mathcal{M}_{2^{m},2^{n}}(K)
as matrices with rows and columns indexed by the subsets of
[m]
and
[n]
, respectively. Orderings on the rows and columns are not needed unless explicitly writing down matrices, in which case we shall be careful to specify such orders. The following definition will be crucial.
In all that follows, when we say that a set is a union of sets from a particular collection of sets, we include the possibility that this union is empty.

Definition 3.1
.

Given a (non-trivial) semiring
K
, we define the mapping

\varphi=\varphi_{K}:\mathcal{P}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}(K):a\mapsto\overline{a},

where for each
a\in\mathcal{P}_{m,n}
the matrix
\overline{a}\in\mathcal{M}_{2^{m},2^{n}}(K)
is defined, for
X\subseteq[m]
and
Y\subseteq[n]
, by

\overline{a}_{X,Y}=\begin{cases}1&\mbox{ if }X\cup Y^{\prime}\mbox{ is a (possibly empty) union of }a\mbox{-blocks},\\
0&\mbox{ else.}\end{cases}

Example 3.2
.

Consider the following partitions, all from
\mathcal{P}_{2}
:
